=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
A "Snowflake Tree" is defined as a tree that can be generated by the following procedure:

- Choose positive integers x,y.
- Prepare one vertex.
- Prepare x more vertices, and connect each of them to the vertex prepared in step 2.
- For each of the x vertices prepared in step 3, attach y leaves to it.

The figure below shows a Snowflake Tree with x=4,y=2. The vertices prepared in steps 2, 3, 4 are shown in red, blue, and green, respectively.

You are given a tree T with N vertices. The vertices are numbered 1 to N, and the i-th edge (i=1,2,\dots,N-1) connects vertices u_i and v_i.
Consider deleting zero or more vertices of T and the edges adjacent to them so that the remaining graph becomes a single Snowflake Tree. Find the minimum number of vertices that must be deleted. Under the constraints of this problem, it is always possible to transform T into a Snowflake Tree.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
u_1 v_1
u_2 v_2
\vdots
u_{N-1} v_{N-1}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 3 \leq N \leq 3 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq u_i < v_i \leq N
- The given graph is a tree.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

8
1 3
2 3
3 4
4 5
5 6
5 7
4 8

Sample Output 1

1

By deleting vertex 8, the given tree can be transformed into a Snowflake Tree with x=2,y=2.

Sample Input 2

3
1 2
2 3

Sample Output 2

0

The given tree is already a Snowflake Tree with x=1,y=1.

Sample Input 3

10
1 3
1 2
5 7
6 10
2 8
1 6
8 9
2 7
1 4

Sample Output 3

3

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Thus algorithm:
1. Read N, edges, build adjacency list of neighbors (ints).
2. Compute deg[v] = len(adj[v]).
3. For each vertex v, create list L = [deg[to] for to in adj[v]].
4. Sort L descending.
5. Iterate over L to compute max q * count_ge(q)+1. Need count_ge(q) = number of elements in L >= q. Since sorted descending, we can iterate unique q values. For each q in L (maybe duplicates), count_ge(q) is index of first element <=? Let's implement:
   L sorted descending.
   i=0
   while i < len(L):
       q = L[i]
       # count of elements >= q: since sorted descending, all from 0 up to last >= q.
       # Because duplicates equal q; we need count all >= q, not just group of q. If we iterate unique groups descending, count_ge(q) = i + count of duplicates? Let's think.
Sorted descending: e.g. [4,2,2,1]. At i=0 q=4, count_ge=1. At i=1 q=2, count_ge=3 (elements >=2 are indices 0,1,2). Let's examine the snowflake definition more: "Choose positive integers x,y. Prepare one vertex. Prepare x more vertices, and connect each of them to the vertex prepared in step 2. Implementation:
import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
    for _ in range(N-1):
        u,v = map(int,input().split())
        u-=1; v-=1
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    deg = [len(adj[i]) for i in range(N)]
    ans = 0
    for v in range(N):
        lst = [deg[to] for to in adj[v]]
        lst.sort(reverse=True)
        i=0
        m=len(lst)
        while i<m:
            q=lst[i]
            j=i+1
            while j<m and lst[j]==q:
                j+=1
            if q >= 2:
                cnt = j
                val = q * cnt + 1
                if val > ans:
                    ans = val
            i=j
    print(N - ans)
if __name__=="__main__": main()

Now, could a selected "leaf" at distance 2 from r be adjacent to another selected middle's leaf? That would create a path between leaves not through r, causing cycle? In a tree, if l1 adjacent to l2 and l1-m1-r-m2-l2, then cycle l1-m1-r-m2-l2-l1, impossible. So no.

Now, let's think about if a selected middle m could have a selected leaf l that is also adjacent to an unselected vertex w, and w is adjacent to r? That would be cycle r-m-l-w-r if w neighbor r, impossible. If w not selected, no issue.

Thus original degrees of leaves not relevant. Our formula only uses degrees of potential middle vertices, not leaves. Good.

Deleting vertices and adjacent edges: If you delete a vertex, all incident edges removed. Edges between remaining vertices remain. So yes induced subgraph on remaining vertices. Good.

Now, let's think about if a selected middle m could be adjacent to another selected middle m'? That would cycle r-m-m'-r. So no.

Thus construction valid.

Now, let's think about if there is a possibility of memory issue with `adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]` and appending ints. If memory limit is 1024 MB, fine. If 256 MB, maybe still fine. Let's estimate more carefully:
- Outer list: 300k references = 2.4 MB.
- Empty list objects: 300k * 56 = 16.8 MB.
- Each edge append: two references in lists. Each list has overallocated array. Total references capacity maybe ~2E * 8 = 4.8 MB plus overallocation overhead maybe ~8 MB. The int objects: When parsing `u,v = map(int, input().split())`, `u` and `v` are Python ints. We append `v` to adj[u] and `u` to adj[v]. The same int object `u` is appended to adj[v]; `v` appended to adj[u]. For each edge, two int objects from parsing are stored. So 2*(N-1) int objects ~600k * 28 = 16.8 MB. But wait, for vertex labels >256, each occurrence is a separate int object? The input parsing creates new int objects for each line; if the same label appears in multiple lines, each line creates a new int object. So adjacency stores references to these per-occurrence int objects. There are 2E = 600k int objects. Yes 16.8 MB. Total ~16.8+2.4+16.8+4.8+overhead ~45 MB. Degree list: 300k ints. But degrees are computed as len(adj[i]) returns new int? Actually len returns int object; stored in list. 300k ints ~8.4 MB + list refs 2.4 MB = 10.8 MB. Total ~60 MB.
